So applying L’Hospital’s Rule once would give us lim (x→0) [cos x]/1. Now substituting x=0 into this gives cos(0)/1, which is 1/1 = 1. So the limit should be 1.

Another way without using L’Hospital or series is by using the squeeze theorem. The idea is that for small angles near 0, sin(x) is approximately equal to x, and as x approaches 0, their ratio approaches 1. But more rigorously, we can use inequalities involving sine functions.

I remember that for 0 < |x| < π/2, cos x ≤ (sin x)/x ≤ 1. Since as x approaches 0, cos x approaches 1, the middle term (sin x)/x is squeezed between two expressions both approaching 1. Therefore, by the squeeze theorem, lim (x→0) (sin x)/x = 1.

So all these methods point towards the same answer: the limit is 1.

To evaluate the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \), I'll start by simplifying the expression. 

I notice that both terms have a common factor of \( x \), so I can factor it out from inside the square root:

\[
\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} = x \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}}
\]

Now, substitute this back into the original expression:

\[
\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x = x \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} - x
\]

Factor out \( x \) from both terms:

\[
= x \left( \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} - 1 \right)
\]

To simplify further, I'll use the approximation that for small values of \( \frac{2}{x} \), \( \sqrt{1 + a} \) can be approximated by \( 1 + \frac{a}{2} \). Applying this:

\[
\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} \approx 1 + \frac{1}{x}
\]

Substitute the approximation back into the expression:

\[
= x \left( 1 + \frac{1}{x} - 1 \right) = x \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 1
\]

Therefore, as \( x \) approaches infinity, the limit of the given expression is 1.
</think>

To evaluate the limit:

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \left( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \right)
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Factor Out \( x \) from Inside the Square Root:**
   
   We can factor out \( x \) to simplify the expression inside the square root:
   \[
   \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} = \sqrt{x^2\left(1 + \frac{2}{x}\right)} = |x|\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}}
   \]
   
   Since \( x \to \infty \), we can assume \( x > 0 \) and thus:
   \[
   \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} = x\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}}
   \]

2. **Express the Original Expression:**
   
   Substitute back into the original limit expression:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \left( x\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} - x \right)
   \]
   
3. **Factor Out \( x \) from Both Terms:**
   
   Factor out \( x \):
   \[
   = \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \left[ x \left( \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} - 1 \right) \right]
   \]
   
4. **Simplify the Expression Inside the Limit:**
   
   To simplify \( \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} - 1 \), multiply and divide by the conjugate:
   \[
   \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} - 1 = \frac{\left( \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} - 1 \right) \left( \sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1 \right)}{\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1}
   \]
   
   The numerator becomes:
   \[
   (1 + \frac{2}{x}) - 1 = \frac{2}{x}
   \]
   
   So, we have:
   \[
   \frac{\frac{2}{x}}{\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1}
   \]
   
5. **Substitute Back into the Limit Expression:**
   
   Now, substitute back:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} x \cdot \left( \frac{\frac{2}{x}}{\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1} \right) = \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1}
   \]
   
6. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   
   As \( x \to \infty \), \( \frac{2}{x} \) approaches 0:
   \[
   = \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + 0} + 1} = \frac{2}{2} = 1
   \]
   
**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{1}
\]

\[

\]

   - As \( x \to 0 \), \( \tan(x) \to 0 \).
   - Similarly, \( x \to 0 \).
   - Thus, the limit is in the indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \).

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   \[

   \]
   
3. **Differentiate Numerator and Denominator:**
   - \( \frac{d}{dx}[\tan x] = \sec^2 x \)
   - \( \frac{d}{dx}[x] = 1 \)

   \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sec^2 x}{1} = \sec^2(0) = (1)^2 = 1
   \]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Form of the Limit:**
   
   As \( x \) approaches 0:
   - The numerator \( \sin(2x) \) approaches \( \sin(0) = 0 \).
   - The denominator \( x \) also approaches 0.

   Thus, the limit is in the indeterminate form \( \frac{0}{0} \).

2. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule:**
   
   L'Hospital's Rule states that if \( \lim_{{x \to c}} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} \) results in an indeterminate form of \( \frac{0}{0} \) or \( \frac{\infty}{\infty} \), then:
   
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to c}} \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} = \lim_{{x \to c}} \frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}
   \]
   
   provided the limit on the right-hand side exists.

3. **Differentiate the Numerator and Denominator:**
   
   - \( f(x) = \sin(2x) \)
     \[
     f'(x) = 2\cos(2x)
     \]
     
   - \( g(x) = x \)
     \[
     g'(x) = 1
     \]

4. **Compute the New Limit:**
   
   Applying L'Hospital's Rule:
   
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin(2x)}{x} = \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{2\cos(2x)}{1}
   \]
   
5. **Evaluate the Simplified Limit:**
   
   Substitute \( x = 0 \):
   
   \[
   2\cos(0) = 2 \times 1 = 2
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{2}
\]

4. **Apply the Known Limit:** Since this substitution aligns perfectly with the known result, we can directly conclude that the limit is e.

   - Take natural logarithm on both sides: ln(y) = x * ln(1 + 1/x).
   - Expand ln(1 + 1/x) using Taylor series for small t=1/x: ln(1 + t) ≈ t - t²/2 + ...

First, observe that as \( x \) becomes very large, the highest power terms dominate. In this case, the dominant term in both the numerator and the denominator is \( x^2 \).

To simplify the expression, divide both the numerator and the denominator by \( x^2 \), which gives:
\[

\]

As \( x \) approaches infinity, the terms \( \frac{1}{x} \) and \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) approach zero. This simplifies the expression to:
\[

\]

To find the limit of the expression \(\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1}\), we analyze the behavior of both the numerator and the denominator as \( x \) approaches infinity.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Dominant Terms:**
   - For large values of \( x \), the highest degree terms in the numerator and denominator dominate.
   - In the numerator, the dominant term is \( x^2 \).
   - In the denominator, the dominant term is also \( x^2 \).

2. **Divide Numerator and Denominator by \( x^2 \):**
   To simplify the expression, divide both the numerator and the denominator by \( x^2 \):
   \[
   \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} = \frac{1 + \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{1 - \frac{1}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}
   \]

3. **Evaluate the Limit as \( x \to \infty \):**
   As \( x \) approaches infinity, the terms \( \frac{1}{x} \) and \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) approach zero:
   \[
   \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{1 + 0 + 0}{1 - 0 + 0} = \frac{1}{1} = 1
   \]

Maybe I can use L'Hospital's Rule here because both the numerator and denominator are approaching infinity as x goes to infinity. When we have an indeterminate form of type ∞/∞, L'Hospital's Rule says we can take the derivative of the numerator and the derivative of the denominator separately and then take the limit.

So, let's compute the derivatives. The derivative of ln x is 1/x, right? And the derivative of x with respect to x is just 1. So applying L'Hospital's Rule once gives us:

lim (x→∞) (1/x)/1 = lim (x→∞) 1/x

Now, as x approaches infinity, 1/x clearly approaches zero because the denominator grows without bound while the numerator stays at 1.

lim (t→∞) t / e^t

Now, I know that e^t grows much faster than t, so this limit should be zero.

The limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \frac{\ln x}{x} \) can be evaluated by recognizing that the natural logarithm grows much slower than linear functions. Applying L'Hospital's Rule, which involves taking derivatives of the numerator and denominator separately when both approach infinity, we find:

\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{\ln x}{x} = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{1/x}{1} = \lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{1}{x} = 0
\]

Thus, the limit is \( 0 \).

\[
\lim_{{x \to 1}} \frac{\sqrt[3]{x^3 + 1} - 1}{x - 1}

**Analysis:**

1. **Numerator Evaluation at \( x = 1 \):**
   Substituting \( x = 1 \) into the numerator:
   \[
   \sqrt[3]{1^3 + 1} - 1 = \sqrt[3]{2} - 1 \approx 0.2599
   \]
   
2. **Denominator at \( x = 1 \):**
   The denominator becomes zero:
   \[
   1 - 1 = 0
   \]

3. **Approaching from Different Sides:**
   - **Right-Hand Limit (\( x \to 1^+ \)):** As \( x \) approaches 1 from the right, both numerator and denominator are positive (denominator approaching zero positively), leading to:
     \[
     +\infty
     \]
     
   - **Left-Hand Limit (\( x \to 1^- \)):** Here, the denominator is negative as \( x < 1 \). The numerator remains approximately 0.2599 (positive), so:
     \[
     -\infty
     \]

4. **Conclusion:**
   Since the right-hand limit and left-hand limit are not equal, the overall two-sided limit does not exist.

**Final Answer:**

The limit does not exist as it approaches positive infinity from one side and negative infinity from the other.

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \), we can follow these steps:

### Step 1: Rewrite the Function
First, express the function in a form that makes differentiation easier:
\[
f(x) = x^{-1}
\]

### Step 2: Apply the Power Rule
The power rule states that if \( f(x) = x^n \), then the derivative \( f'(x) = n \cdot x^{n-1} \). Applying this to our function:

\[
f'(x) = -1 \cdot x^{-1 - 1} = -x^{-2}
\]

### Step 3: Simplify the Expression
Rewrite the expression back in terms of fractions:
\[
f'(x) = -\frac{1}{x^2}
\]

### Final Answer
The derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \) is:
\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{1}{x^{2}}}
\]

To find the derivative of the function:

\[
f(x) = x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1
\]

we'll differentiate each term separately using basic differentiation rules. Here's how it's done step by step:

---

### Step 1: Differentiate \( x^3 \)
Using the **power rule**, which states that \(\frac{d}{dx} x^n = n x^{n-1}\):

\[
\frac{d}{dx} x^3 = 3x^{2}
\]

---

### Step 2: Differentiate \( 2x^2 \)
Apply the **constant multiple rule** and then the power rule:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} (2x^2) = 2 \cdot \frac{d}{dx} x^2 = 2 \cdot 2x^{1} = 4x
\]

---

### Step 3: Differentiate \( x \)
The derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} x = 1
\]

---

### Step 4: Differentiate the constant term \( 1 \)
The derivative of a constant is always zero:

\[
\frac{d}{dx} 1 = 0
\]

---

### Combine All Derivatives
Now, add up all the derivatives from each step:

\[
f'(x) = 3x^{2} + 4x + 1 + 0 = 3x^{2} + 4x + 1
\]

---

### Final Answer

\[
\boxed{3x^2 + 4x + 1}
\]

The derivative of \( \tan x \) with respect to \( x \) is found using the quotient rule because \( \tan x = \frac{\sin x}{\cos x} \). Applying the quotient rule:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{\sin x}{\cos x}\right) = \frac{\cos x \cdot \cos x - \sin x \cdot (-\sin x)}{\cos^2 x} = \frac{\cos^2 x + \sin^2 x}{\cos^2 x}
\]

Using the Pythagorean identity \( \cos^2 x + \sin^2 x = 1 \), this simplifies to:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{1}{\cos^2 x} = \sec^2 x
\]

**Final Answer:** The derivative of \( F(x) = \tan x \) is \( \sec^2 x \).

\[

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \), we'll use the **Product Rule** from calculus. The Product Rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = f(x) \cdot g(x) \), then the derivative \( h'(x) \) is:

\[
h'(x) = f'(x) \cdot g(x) + f(x) \cdot g'(x)
\]

**Step 1: Identify the Components**

Let:
- \( f(x) = \cos x \)
- \( g(x) = \ln x \)

**Step 2: Find the Derivatives of Each Component**

First, find the derivative of \( f(x) = \cos x \):
\[
f'(x) = -\sin x
\]

Next, find the derivative of \( g(x) = \ln x \):
\[
g'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
\]

**Step 3: Apply the Product Rule**

Substitute these derivatives back into the Product Rule formula:
\[
F'(x) = f'(x) \cdot g(x) + f(x) \cdot g'(x)
\]
\[
F'(x) = (-\sin x) \cdot \ln x + \cos x \cdot \left( \frac{1}{x} \right)
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the Expression**

Combine the terms to get the final derivative:
\[
F'(x) = -(\sin x)(\ln x) + \frac{\cos x}{x}
\]

**Final Answer**
The derivative of \( F(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \) is:

\[
F'(x) = -(\sin x)(\ln x) + \frac{\cos x}{x}
\]

   \[
   \ln(y) = \ln(x^x)
   \]
   
2. **Simplify Using Logarithm Properties:**
   \[
   \ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
   \]

3. **Differentiate Both Sides with Respect to \( x \):**
   - Left Side:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}[\ln(y)] = \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx}
     \]
   - Right Side (using the product rule):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}[x \cdot \ln(x)] = \ln(x) + 1
     \]

4. **Set the Derivatives Equal and Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):**
   \[
   \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1
   \]
   Multiply both sides by \( y \):
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = y \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]

5. **Substitute \( y = x^x \) Back into the Equation:**
   \[
   \frac{dy}{dx} = x^x \cdot (\ln(x) + 1)
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{x^{x}(\ln (x)+1)}
\]

     \[
     F'(x) = u'v + uv'
     \]

     \[

     \]

**Solution:**

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we can use the **chain rule**.

1. **Rewrite the Function:**
   
   First, express \( F(x) \) in a form that's easier to differentiate:
   \[
   F(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1}
   \]

2. **Apply the Chain Rule:**
   
   The chain rule states that if you have a function composed of two functions, say \( f(g(x)) \), its derivative is:
   \[
   f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x)
   \]
   
   Here, let \( u = x^2 + 1 \) and \( F(u) = u^{-1} \).

3. **Differentiate the Outer Function:**
   
   Differentiate \( F(u) = u^{-1} \) with respect to \( u \):
   \[
   \frac{dF}{du} = -u^{-2}
   \]

4. **Differentiate the Inner Function:**
   
   Now, differentiate \( u = x^2 + 1 \) with respect to \( x \):
   \[
   \frac{du}{dx} = 2x
   \]

5. **Combine Using the Chain Rule:**
   
   Multiply the derivatives together:
   \[
   \frac{dF}{dx} = \frac{dF}{du} \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = -u^{-2} \cdot 2x
   \]
   
6. **Substitute Back \( u = x^2 + 1 \):**
   
   Replace \( u \) with the original inner function:
   \[
   \frac{dF}{dx} = - (x^2 + 1)^{-2} \cdot 2x
   \]
   
7. **Simplify the Expression:**
   
   Simplify by combining constants and exponents:
   \[
   F'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{-\dfrac{2x}{(x^{2}+1)^{2}}}

Next, I'll differentiate each part separately. The derivative of \( e^u \) with respect to \( u \) is still \( e^u \), and the derivative of \( u = x^2 \) with respect to \( x \) is \( 2x \).

To find the derivative of \( e^{x^2} \) with respect to \( x \), we'll use the **chain rule**. The chain rule is a fundamental technique in calculus for differentiating composite functions.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Identify the Composite Functions:**
   - Let’s consider the outer function as \( e^{u} \), where \( u = x^2 \).

2. **Differentiate the Outer Function:**
   - The derivative of \( e^{u} \) with respect to \( u \) is \( e^{u} \).
     \[
     \frac{d}{du} (e^{u}) = e^{u}
     \]

3. **Differentiate the Inner Function:**
   - Now, differentiate \( u = x^2 \) with respect to \( x \).
     \[
     \frac{du}{dx} = 2x
     \]

4. **Apply the Chain Rule:**
   - Multiply the derivatives of the outer and inner functions.
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} (e^{x^2}) = e^{u} \cdot \frac{du}{dx} = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x
     \]

5. **Final Answer:**
   - Simplify the expression to get the final derivative.
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} (e^{x^2}) = 2x e^{x^2}
     \]

### Final Answer:
\boxed{2x e^{x^{2}}}

Now for the denominator, g(x) = x squared. The derivative of x² is 2x. Okay, so far I have f'(x) = 1/x and g'(x) = 2x.

Putting these into the quotient rule formula: F'(x) = [f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)] / [g(x)]². Plugging in the values, that becomes [(1/x)(x²) - (ln x)(2x)] divided by (x squared) squared.

So putting it all together, the derivative F'(x) is [x - 2x ln x] divided by x⁴.

I think this is the correct derivative then. So F'(x) = (1 - 2 ln x)/x³.

The function \( F(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} \) can be differentiated using the quotient rule.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify f(x) and g(x):**
   - Numerator (\( f(x) \)): \( \ln x \)
   - Denominator (\( g(x) \)): \( x^2 \)

2. **Compute Derivatives:**
   - \( f'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \)
   - \( g'(x) = 2x \)

3. **Apply the Quotient Rule:**
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)}{[g(x)]^2} = \frac{\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)(x^2) - (\ln x)(2x)}{(x^2)^2}
   \]

4. **Simplify Numerator and Denominator:**
   - Numerator:
     \[
     \left(\frac{1}{x}\right)x^2 = x
     \]
     \[
     (\ln x)(2x) = 2x \ln x
     \]
     So, the numerator becomes \( x - 2x \ln x \).
   - Denominator:
     \[
     (x^2)^2 = x^4
     \]

5. **Factor and Simplify:**
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{x(1 - 2 \ln x)}{x^4} = \frac{1 - 2 \ln x}{x^3}
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
The derivative of \( F(x) \) is \( \frac{1 - 2\ln x}{x^3} \).

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \ln(\sin x) \), we'll use the **chain rule**. The chain rule is a formula for computing the derivative of the composition of two or more functions. In this case, our outer function is the natural logarithm (\( \ln \)) and the inner function is \( \sin x \).

Here's how to apply the chain rule step by step:

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions**:
   - Outer function: \( \ln(u) \), where \( u = \sin x \)
   - Inner function: \( u = \sin x \)

2. **Differentiate the outer function with respect to the inner function**:
   \[
   \frac{d}{du} [\ln(u)] = \frac{1}{u}
   \]

3. **Differentiate the inner function with respect to \( x \)**:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} [\sin x] = \cos x
   \]

4. **Multiply the derivatives together** (chain rule):
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{1}{\sin x} \cdot \cos x
   \]

5. **Simplify the expression**:
   \[
   F'(x) = \cot x
   \]
   Where \( \cot x \) is the cotangent of \( x \).

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\cot(x)}
\]

To find the **second derivative** of the function \( F(x) = x^3 \ln x \), follow these steps:

1. **First Derivative (Using Product Rule):**

   The product rule states that if \( u(x) \cdot v(x) \), then:
   
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx}[u(x) \cdot v(x)] = u'(x) \cdot v(x) + u(x) \cdot v'(x)
   \]
   
   Let’s choose:
   
   - \( u(x) = x^3 \) ⇒ \( u'(x) = 3x^2 \)
   - \( v(x) = \ln x \) ⇒ \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \)

   Applying the product rule:
   
   \[
   F'(x) = 3x^2 \cdot \ln x + x^3 \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2
   \]

2. **Second Derivative:**

   Now, differentiate \( F'(x) \):
   
   \[
   F''(x) = \frac{d}{dx}[3x^2 \ln x] + \frac{d}{dx}[x^2]
   \]
   
   - Differentiate \( 3x^2 \ln x \) using the product rule again:
     
     Let’s let \( u = 3x^2 \) and \( v = \ln x \):
     
     - \( u' = 6x \)
     - \( v' = \frac{1}{x} \)
     
     So,
     
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}[3x^2 \ln x] = 6x \cdot \ln x + 3x^2 \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 6x \ln x + 3x
     \]
   
   - Differentiate \( x^2 \):
     
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}[x^2] = 2x
     \]
   
   Combining both parts:
   
   \[
   F''(x) = 6x \ln x + 3x + 2x = 6x \ln x + 5x
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\[
\boxed{F''(x) = (6 \ln x + 5)x}
\]

In this case, let me identify \( u = e^x \) and \( v = 1 + e^x \).

- \( e^x \cdot e^x = e^{2x} \)

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), we can use the quotient rule. 

Let \( u = e^x \) and \( v = 1 + e^x \).

- \( u' = e^x \)
- \( v' = e^x \)

Applying the quotient rule:
\[

\]

Simplifying the numerator:
\[
e^x (1 + e^x) - e^{2x} = e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x} = e^x
\]

So, the derivative is:
\[

To find the derivative of the function \( F(x) = x \ln x \), we will use the **product rule**. The product rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = f(x) \cdot g(x) \), then its derivative is:

\[
h'(x) = f'(x) \cdot g(x) + f(x) \cdot g'(x)
\]

Applying this to \( F(x) \):

1. **Identify the functions:**
   - Let \( f(x) = x \)
   - Let \( g(x) = \ln x \)

2. **Find the derivatives of each function:**
   - \( f'(x) = 1 \) (since the derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is 1)
   - \( g'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \) (since the derivative of \( \ln x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \frac{1}{x} \))

3. **Apply the product rule:**
   \[
   F'(x) = f'(x) \cdot g(x) + f(x) \cdot g'(x)
   \]
   Substituting the derivatives:
   \[
   F'(x) = 1 \cdot \ln x + x \cdot \frac{1}{x}
   \]

4. **Simplify the expression:**
   \[
   F'(x) = \ln x + 1
   \]
   
**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{\ln(x) + 1}

\[

\]

Let:
- \(u(x) = \sin x\) ⇒ \(u'(x) = \cos x\)
- \(v(x) = x^2 + 1\) ⇒ \(v'(x) = 2x\)

The limit as x approaches 0 of (sin x)/x is a well-known result in calculus.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Problem Statement:** Find lim_{x → 0} (sin x) / x.

2. **Direct Substitution:** If we substitute x = 0 directly, both numerator and denominator become zero, resulting in an indeterminate form 0/0.

3. **Using Taylor Series Expansion of sin x:**
   - The Taylor series expansion of sin x around 0 is: sin x = x - (x³)/6 + (x^5)/120 - ...
   - Divide both sides by x: (sin x)/x = 1 - (x²)/6 + (x^4)/120 - ...

4. **Evaluating the Limit:** As x approaches 0, higher-order terms involving x become negligible.
   - Therefore, lim_{x → 0} (sin x)/x = 1.

5. **Using Squeeze Theorem:**
   - For all x ≠ 0 near 0, |sin x| ≤ |x|.
   - This implies that (sin x)/x is bounded between -1 and 1.
   - As x approaches 0 from both sides, sin x / x approaches 1.

6. **Alternative Approach: L’Hôpital’s Rule**
   - Apply derivatives to numerator and denominator:
     lim_{x → 0} (sin x)/x = lim_{x → 0} (cos x)/1
   - cos(0) = 1, so the limit is 1.

**Final Answer:** The limit is equal to 1.

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of the given rational function:

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Degrees of the Polynomials:**
   - The degree of the numerator is \( 2 \) (from \( 3x^2 \)).
   - The degree of the denominator is also \( 2 \) (from \( 5x^2 \)).

2. **Compare the Leading Coefficients:**
   - Since both polynomials have the same degree, the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity is determined by the ratio of their leading coefficients.
   
3. **Calculate the Limit:**
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2} = \frac{\text{Leading Coefficient of Numerator}}{\text{Leading Coefficient of Denominator}} = \frac{3}{5}
   \]

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin 2x}{x}
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Understand the Problem:**
   We need to evaluate the limit of \(\frac{\sin 2x}{x}\) as \(x\) approaches 0.

2. **Recognize Standard Limits:**
   Recall that:
   \[
   \lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin x}{x} = 1
   \]
   
3. **Apply the Double-Angle Identity:**
   Use the identity \(\sin 2x = 2 \sin x \cos x\):
   \[
   \frac{\sin 2x}{x} = \frac{2 \sin x \cos x}{x}
   \]
   
4. **Simplify the Expression:**
   Break it into two parts:
   \[
   \frac{2 \sin x \cos x}{x} = 2 \left( \frac{\sin x}{x} \right) \cdot (\cos x)
   \]
   
5. **Evaluate Each Part Separately as \(x\) Approaches 0:**
   - \(\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{\sin x}{x} = 1\)
   - \(\lim_{{x \to 0}} \cos x = 1\)
   
6. **Combine the Limits:**
   Multiply the results of each limit:
   \[

   \]

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches 0 of \( \frac{x - \sin x}{x^3} \), I can use the Taylor series expansion for \( \sin x \).

The Taylor series expansion of \( \sin x \) around 0 is:
\( \sin x = x - \frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3) \)

Substituting this into the expression, we get:
\( x - \sin x = x - \left( x - \frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3) \right) = \frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3) \)

Therefore,
\( \frac{x - \sin x}{x^3} = \frac{\frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3)}{x^3} = \frac{1}{6} + o(1) \)

\( \lim_{x \to 0} \frac{x - \sin x}{x^3} = \frac{1}{6} \)

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{x - \sin x}{x^3}
\]

we can use the Taylor series expansion of \(\sin x\) around \(x = 0\).

The Taylor series for \(\sin x\) is:
\[
\sin x = x - \frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3)
\]
where \(o(x^3)\) represents higher-order terms that become negligible as \(x \to 0\).

Subtracting this from \(x\), we get:
\[
x - \sin x = x - \left( x - \frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3) \right) = \frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3)
\]

Now, substitute back into the original expression:
\[
\frac{x - \sin x}{x^3} = \frac{\frac{x^3}{6} + o(x^3)}{x^3} = \frac{1}{6} + \frac{o(x^3)}{x^3}
\]

As \(x \to 0\), the term \(\frac{o(x^3)}{x^3}\) approaches 0. Therefore:
\[
\lim_{{x \to 0}} \frac{x - \sin x}{x^3} = \frac{1}{6}
\]

To evaluate the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \frac{2^x}{x^3} \), I start by analyzing the behavior of both the numerator and the denominator individually.

The numerator is an exponential function, \( 2^x \), which grows at a very fast rate as \( x \) increases. On the other hand, the denominator is a polynomial function, \( x^3 \), which grows at a much slower rate compared to an exponential function.

Considering these growth rates, it's evident that as \( x \) becomes very large, the numerator will dominate and grow significantly faster than the denominator. This suggests that the ratio \( \frac{2^x}{x^3} \) will increase without bound.

To further confirm this intuition, I can apply L'Hospital's Rule since both the numerator and denominator approach infinity as \( x \) approaches infinity. Repeatedly differentiating the numerator and denominator multiple times (specifically three times in this case) results in a limit where the exponential term remains while the polynomial term becomes negligible.

Ultimately, after simplifying through differentiation, it becomes clear that the limit of \( \frac{2^x}{x^3} \) as \( x \) approaches infinity is infinity.

To find the limit:
\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{2^x}{x^3}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Behavior of Numerator and Denominator:**
   - The numerator \( 2^x \) is an exponential function, which grows very rapidly as \( x \) increases.
   - The denominator \( x^3 \) is a polynomial function, which grows at a slower rate compared to an exponential function.

2. **Determine the Dominant Term:**
   - As \( x \) approaches infinity, \( 2^x \) will grow much faster than \( x^3 \). This suggests that the numerator will dominate, leading the entire fraction to increase without bound.

3. **Apply L'Hospital's Rule (If Necessary):**
   - To rigorously confirm this intuition, we can apply L'Hospital's Rule since both the numerator and denominator approach infinity as \( x \to \infty \).
   
4. **Differentiate Numerator and Denominator:**
   - Differentiating multiple times:
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} 2^x = (\ln 2) \cdot 2^x
     \]
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} x^3 = 3x^2
     \]
     Repeating this process three times (since the denominator is a third-degree polynomial):
     - After differentiating both numerator and denominator three times, we get:
       \[
       \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{(\ln 2)^3 \cdot 2^x}{6} = \infty
       \]
   
5. **Conclusion:**
   - The limit evaluates to infinity because the exponential function in the numerator grows much faster than any polynomial term.

The leading term in the numerator is \( x^2 \) with a coefficient of 1.
Similarly, the leading term in the denominator is also \( x^2 \) with a coefficient of 1.

Therefore, as \( x \) approaches infinity, the lower-degree terms become negligible compared to the highest degree terms. This means that the limit simplifies to the ratio of the coefficients of \( x^2 \), which is \( \frac{1}{1} = 1 \).

Thus, the limit evaluates to 1.
</think>

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of the function \( \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} \), follow these steps:

1. **Identify the Degrees of the Polynomials:**
   - The numerator is a quadratic polynomial (degree 2).
   - The denominator is also a quadratic polynomial (degree 2).

2. **Compare the Leading Coefficients:**
   - For both the numerator and the denominator, the leading coefficient (the coefficient of \( x^2 \)) is \( 1 \).

3. **Evaluate the Limit:**
   - When the degrees of the numerator and denominator are equal, the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity is the ratio of their leading coefficients.
   - Therefore, the limit is:
     \[
     \lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} = \frac{1}{1} = 1
     \]

Next, differentiate \( -5x \). Using the same power rule, the derivative is \( -5 \).

**Solution:**

To find the derivative of the function \( F(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we will differentiate each term separately with respect to \( x \).

1. **Differentiate \( 3x^2 \):**
   Using the power rule, which states that \( \frac{d}{dx} x^n = n x^{n-1} \),
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (3x^2) = 3 \times 2x^{2-1} = 6x
   \]

2. **Differentiate \( -5x \):**
   Applying the power rule again,
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (-5x) = -5
   \]

3. **Differentiate the constant term \( 4 \):**
   The derivative of a constant is always zero.
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (4) = 0
   \]

**Combining all the derivatives:**
\[
F'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
\]

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( F(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \) is:
\[
\boxed{6x - 5}
\]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{1}{x} \), we'll use basic differentiation rules.

**Step 1: Rewrite the Function**
First, rewrite \( \frac{1}{x} \) as \( x^{-1} \).

\[ f(x) = x^{-1} \]

**Step 2: Apply the Power Rule**
The power rule states that if \( f(x) = x^n \), then \( f'(x) = n \cdot x^{n-1} \).

Applying this to our function:

\[ f'(x) = -1 \cdot x^{-1 - 1} = -x^{-2} \]

**Step 3: Simplify the Expression**
Rewrite the negative exponent as a fraction.

\[ f'(x) = -\frac{1}{x^2} \]

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) with respect to \( x \) is:

\[
\boxed{-\dfrac{1}{x^{2}}}

The derivative of \( F(x) = \sin x \) with respect to \( x \) is calculated using the fundamental rules of calculus. 

Using the limit definition:
\[

\]

\[

\]
Substituting back and simplifying leads to two limits that evaluate to 0 and 1 respectively.

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1 \), we will apply basic differentiation rules:

1. **Power Rule**: The derivative of \( x^n \) with respect to \( x \) is \( nx^{n-1} \).
2. **Constant Multiple Rule**: The derivative of a constant multiplied by a function is the constant times the derivative of the function.
3. **Sum/Difference Rule**: The derivative of a sum/difference of functions is the sum/difference of their derivatives.

Let's compute each term step by step:

- \( \frac{d}{dx}(x^3) = 3x^{2} \)
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(2x^2) = 4x \)
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(x) = 1 \)
- \( \frac{d}{dx}(1) = 0 \)

Now, combine all the derivatives:

\[
f'(x) = 3x^{2} + 4x + 1
\]

To find the second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \), we'll proceed step by step.

### Step 1: Find the First Derivative
We use the product rule for differentiation, which states that if \( u(x) = x^2 \) and \( v(x) = e^x \), then:
\[
F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]
First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[
u'(x) = 2x
\]
\[
v'(x) = e^x
\]
Now, apply the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
\]

### Step 2: Simplify the First Derivative
Factor out \( e^x \):
\[
F'(x) = e^x (2x + x^2)
\]
\[
F'(x) = e^x (x^2 + 2x)
\]

### Step 3: Find the Second Derivative
Differentiate \( F'(x) \) using the product rule again. Let:
\[
u_1(x) = x^2 + 2x \quad \text{and} \quad v_1(x) = e^x
\]
First, find the derivatives of \( u_1(x) \) and \( v_1(x) \):
\[
u_1'(x) = 2x + 2
\]
\[
v_1'(x) = e^x
\]
Now, apply the product rule:
\[
F''(x) = u_1'(x)v_1(x) + u_1(x)v_1'(x)
\]
\[
F''(x) = (2x + 2)e^x + (x^2 + 2x)e^x
\]

### Step 4: Simplify the Second Derivative
Combine like terms:
\[
F''(x) = e^x (2x + 2 + x^2 + 2x)
\]
\[
F''(x) = e^x (x^2 + 4x + 2)
\]

### Final Answer
The second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \) is:
\[
\boxed{e^{x} \left(x^{2} + 4 x + 2\right)}
\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), we'll use the chain rule.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Rewrite the Function:**
   \[
   F(x) = (x^2 + 1)^{-1}
   \]

2. **Apply the Chain Rule:**
   - Let \( u = x^2 + 1 \)
   - Then, \( F(u) = u^{-1} \)

3. **Differentiate the Outer Function with Respect to \( u \):**
   \[
   \frac{dF}{du} = -u^{-2}
   \]

4. **Differentiate the Inner Function with Respect to \( x \):**
   \[
   \frac{du}{dx} = 2x
   \]

5. **Multiply the Derivatives Together:**
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{dF}{du} \times \frac{du}{dx} = -u^{-2} \times 2x = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{-\dfrac{2x}{(x^{2}+1)^{2}}}

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions**:
   - Let \( u = x^2 \) (inner function).
   - The outer function is \( e^u \).

2. **Differentiate the outer function with respect to \( u \)**:
   \[
   \frac{d}{du} (e^u) = e^u
   \]

3. **Differentiate the inner function with respect to \( x \)**:
   \[
   \frac{du}{dx} = 2x
   \]

4. **Apply the chain rule**:
   \[

   \]

5. **Simplify the expression**:

   \]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} \), I will use the quotient rule.

First, identify the numerator and denominator:
- Numerator (\( u \)) = \( \ln x \)
- Denominator (\( v \)) = \( x^2 \)

Next, find the derivatives of \( u \) and \( v \):
- \( u' = \frac{1}{x} \)
- \( v' = 2x \)

Apply the quotient rule:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2}
\]
Substitute the values into the formula:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{\left( \frac{1}{x} \right)(x^2) - (\ln x)(2x)}{(x^2)^2}
\]

Simplify each term in the numerator:
- \( \left( \frac{1}{x} \right)(x^2) = x \)
- \( (\ln x)(2x) = 2x \ln x \)

So, the expression becomes:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{x - 2x \ln x}{x^4}
\]

Factor out an \( x \) from the numerator:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{x(1 - 2 \ln x)}{x^4}
\]
Simplify by canceling one \( x \):
\[
F'(x) = \frac{1 - 2 \ln x}{x^3}
\]

The final derivative is:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{1 - 2 \ln x}{x^3}
\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \ln(\sin x) \), we'll use the **chain rule**. The chain rule states that if you have a composite function \( f(g(x)) \), its derivative is \( f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x) \).

Here, our outer function is the natural logarithm (\( \ln(u) \)), and the inner function is \( u = \sin x \). 

1. **Differentiate the outer function**:  
   The derivative of \( \ln(u) \) with respect to \( u \) is \( \frac{1}{u} \).

2. **Differentiate the inner function**:  
   The derivative of \( \sin x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \cos x \).

3. **Apply the chain rule**:  
   Multiply the derivatives obtained from steps 1 and 2.

\[
F'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} [\ln(\sin x)] = \frac{1}{\sin x} \cdot \cos x
\]

4. **Simplify the expression**:
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{\cos x}{\sin x}
   \]
   
5. **Final result**:  
   This simplifies to \( \cot x \), since \( \frac{\cos x}{\sin x} = \cot x \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

   - \( f'(x) = e^x \)

The derivative of \(\frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1}\) can be found using the quotient rule. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \(h(x) = \frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\), its derivative is given by:

\[
h'(x) = \frac{f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)}{[g(x)]^2}
\]

Here, let:
- \(f(x) = \sin x\) 
- \(g(x) = x^2 + 1\)

First, find the derivatives of \(f(x)\) and \(g(x)\):
- \(f'(x) = \cos x\)
- \(g'(x) = 2x\)

\[
h'(x) = \frac{\cos x (x^2 + 1) - \sin x (2x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

So, the derivative is:

\[
\boxed{\frac{\cos x (x^2 + 1) - 2x \sin x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}}

To find the derivative of the function \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( F(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

- Let \( v(x) = 1 + x^2 \)

- \( v'(x) = 2x \)

**Step 4:** Simplify the numerator.

(1)(1 + x^2) = 1 + x^2

(x)(2x) = 2x^2

To find the derivative of \(\sec{x}\), we'll use the differentiation rules for reciprocal functions.

**Step 1:** Recognize that \(\sec{x} = \frac{1}{\cos{x}}\).

**Step 2:** Apply the quotient rule. If \(f(x) = \frac{g(x)}{h(x)}\), then
\[
f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2}
\]
Here, \(g(x) = 1\) and \(h(x) = \cos{x}\).

**Step 3:** Compute the derivatives:
- \(g'(x) = 0\)
- \(h'(x) = -\sin{x}\)

**Step 4:** Substitute into the quotient rule formula:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{0 \cdot \cos{x} - 1 \cdot (-\sin{x})}{(\cos{x})^2} = \frac{\sin{x}}{\cos^2{x}}
\]

**Step 5:** Simplify using trigonometric identities:
\[
\frac{\sin{x}}{\cos^2{x}} = \sec{x}\tan{x}
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\sec{x}\,\tan{x}}
\]

5. **Evaluate at x=0:** cos(0) = 1.

6. **Conclusion:** Therefore, lim_{x→0} [sin(x)/x] = 1.

I need to find the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \).

So, approximately, the square root simplifies to \( \sqrt{x^2} = |x| \), which is just \( x \) since we're dealing with positive values as \( x \) approaches infinity.

This suggests that \( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \) is similar to \( x - x = 0 \). However, I suspect there's a more precise way to evaluate this limit without relying solely on approximation.

To get a better understanding, I'll rationalize the expression by multiplying and dividing by the conjugate:

\[
\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x = \frac{(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x)(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x)}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
\]

Simplifying the numerator:

\[
(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x})^2 - x^2 = (x^2 + 2x) - x^2 = 2x
\]

\[
\frac{2x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
\]

\]

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x \), we'll evaluate the expression step by step.

**Step 1: Rationalize the Expression**

We start by rationalizing the expression to simplify it. Multiply and divide by the conjugate:

\[
\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x = \frac{(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} - x)(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x)}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
\]

**Step 2: Simplify the Numerator**

Expand the numerator:

\[
(\sqrt{x^2 + 2x})^2 - x^2 = (x^2 + 2x) - x^2 = 2x
\]

So, the expression becomes:

\[
\frac{2x}{\sqrt{x^2 + 2x} + x}
\]

**Step 3: Factor Out \( x \) from the Denominator**

Factor out \( x \) inside the square root in the denominator:

\[
\sqrt{x^2(1 + \frac{2}{x})} = x\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}}
\]

Substitute back into the expression:

\[
\frac{2x}{x\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + x}
\]

**Step 4: Simplify Further**

Factor \( x \) from the denominator terms:

\[
\frac{2x}{x(\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1)} = \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + \frac{2}{x}} + 1}
\]

**Step 5: Evaluate the Limit as \( x \) Approaches Infinity**

As \( x \to \infty \), \( \frac{2}{x} \to 0 \):

\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{2}{\sqrt{1 + 0} + 1} = \frac{2}{1 + 1} = \frac{2}{2} = 1
\]

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of the function \( \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} \), I first examine the highest degree terms in both the numerator and the denominator.

Both the numerator and the denominator have an \( x^2 \) term as their highest degree. As \( x \) becomes very large, these dominant quadratic terms will significantly influence the behavior of the function.

By dividing both the numerator and the denominator by \( x^2 \), I can simplify the expression:

\[

\]

\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{1 + 0 + 0}{1 - 0 + 0} = \frac{1}{1} = 1
\]

lim (x→infty) (ln x)/x = lim (x→infty) [d/dx(ln x)] / [d/dx(x)]

The derivative of ln x is 1/x, and the derivative of x is 1. So that gives us:

lim (x→infty) (1/x) / 1 = lim (x→infty) 1/x

As x approaches infinity, 1/x approaches zero. Therefore, the limit should be zero.

I think this makes sense because ln x grows logarithmically, which is much slower than the linear growth of x. So as x becomes very large, dividing it by a function that's growing much slower will indeed make the whole expression approach zero.
</think>

The limit of (ln x)/x as x approaches infinity is 0 because the denominator grows much faster than the numerator.

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we'll apply basic differentiation rules.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Differentiate each term separately:**
   
   - The derivative of \( 3x^2 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 6x
     \]
     
   - The derivative of \( -5x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
     
   - The derivative of the constant term \( 4 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives:**
   
   Adding up the derivatives of each term gives:
   \[

   \]

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = x^3 + 2x^2 + x + 1 \), we'll apply basic differentiation rules to each term individually.

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Differentiate \( x^3 \):**
   
   Using the power rule, which states that \( \frac{d}{dx} x^n = nx^{n-1} \),
   
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} x^3 = 3x^2
   \]

2. **Differentiate \( 2x^2 \):**
   
   Applying the power rule,
   
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (2x^2) = 2 \cdot 2x^{2-1} = 4x
   \]

3. **Differentiate \( x \):**
   
   The derivative of \( x \) with respect to \( x \) is,
   
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} x = 1
   \]

4. **Differentiate the constant term \( 1 \):**
   
   The derivative of any constant is,
   
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (1) = 0
   \]

5. **Combine all derivatives:**

   Adding up all the individual derivatives:
   
   \[
   f'(x) = 3x^2 + 4x + 1
   \]

### Final Answer:

\[
\boxed{3x^{2}+4x+1}
\]

The derivative of \( F(x) = \tan x \) can be found using the quotient rule or by recalling that the derivative of \( \tan x \) is \( \sec^2 x \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**
1. Recognize that \( \tan x = \frac{\sin x}{\cos x} \).
2. Apply the quotient rule:
   - Let \( f(x) = \sin x \) and \( g(x) = \cos x \).
   - Then, \( f'(x) = \cos x \) and \( g'(x) = -\sin x \).
3. Using the quotient rule formula: 
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)}{[g(x)]^2} = \frac{\cos x \cdot \cos x - (-\sin x) \cdot \sin x}{(\cos x)^2}
   \]
4. Simplify the numerator:
   \[
   \cos^2 x + \sin^2 x = 1
   \]
5. Therefore, \( F'(x) = \frac{1}{(\cos x)^2} = \sec^2 x \).

**Final Answer:** {Final Answer: "sec^2(x)"}

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \), we will use the product rule from calculus. The product rule states that if you have a function which is the product of two other functions, say \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \), then the derivative is:

\[
F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify the Functions:**
   - Let \( u(x) = \cos x \)
   - Let \( v(x) = \ln x \)

2. **Find the Derivatives of Each Function:**
   - The derivative of \( u(x) = \cos x \) with respect to \( x \) is:
     \[
     u'(x) = -\sin x
     \]
   - The derivative of \( v(x) = \ln x \) with respect to \( x \) is:
     \[
     v'(x) = \frac{1}{x}
     \]

3. **Apply the Product Rule:**
   \[
   F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
   \]
   Substituting the derivatives found:
   \[
   F'(x) = (-\sin x)(\ln x) + (\cos x)\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)
   \]

4. **Simplify the Expression:**
   \[
   F'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x}
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( F(x) = \cos x \cdot \ln x \) is:

\[
F'(x) = -\sin x \cdot \ln x + \frac{\cos x}{x}

To find the second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \), we'll use differentiation rules step by step.

**Step 1: Find the First Derivative**

We start by finding the first derivative \( F'(x) \).

Given:
\[
F(x) = x^2 e^x
\]

Apply the product rule for differentiation, which states that if \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \) are differentiable functions, then:
\[
\frac{d}{dx}[u(x)v(x)] = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]

Let:
- \( u(x) = x^2 \) ⇒ \( u'(x) = 2x \)
- \( v(x) = e^x \) ⇒ \( v'(x) = e^x \)

Applying the product rule:
\[
F'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
\]

**Step 2: Find the Second Derivative**

Now, we'll find the second derivative \( F''(x) \) by differentiating \( F'(x) \).

Given:
\[
F'(x) = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
\]

Differentiate each term separately using the product rule again.

**First Term: \( 2x e^x \)**

Let:
- \( u_1(x) = 2x \) ⇒ \( u_1'(x) = 2 \)
- \( v_1(x) = e^x \) ⇒ \( v_1'(x) = e^x \)

Applying the product rule:
\[
\frac{d}{dx}[2x e^x] = 2 e^x + 2x e^x
\]

**Second Term: \( x^2 e^x \)**

Let:
- \( u_2(x) = x^2 \) ⇒ \( u_2'(x) = 2x \)
- \( v_2(x) = e^x \) ⇒ \( v_2'(x) = e^x \)

Applying the product rule:
\[
\frac{d}{dx}[x^2 e^x] = 2x e^x + x^2 e^x
\]

**Combine Both Derivatives**

Adding both results together:
\[
F''(x) = (2 e^x + 2x e^x) + (2x e^x + x^2 e^x)
\]
\[
F''(x) = 2 e^x + 4x e^x + x^2 e^x
\]

**Final Answer**

The second derivative of \( F(x) = x^2 e^x \) is:
\[
\boxed{F''(x) = (x^2 + 4x + 2)e^x}
\]

F'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}

\[
f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2}
\]

   \[

To find the derivative of F(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x}, I'll start by recognizing that this is a quotient of two functions. Therefore, I should apply the Quotient Rule.

The Quotient Rule states that if we have a function F(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)}, its derivative F'(x) is given by (u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)) divided by [v(x)] squared.

In this case, let me identify u(x) and v(x). Let's set:
- u(x) = e^x
- v(x) = 1 + e^x

Now, I'll find the derivatives of u and v with respect to x.
- The derivative of u(x), which is u'(x), is also e^x because the derivative of e^x is itself.
- The derivative of v(x), which is v'(x), is e^x as well since the derivative of 1 is zero.

Now, applying the Quotient Rule:
F'(x) = [u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)] / [v(x)]²

F'(x) = [e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)] / (1 + e^x)^2

= [e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x}] / (1 + e^x)^2

Notice that the e^{2x} terms cancel out:
= e^x / (1 + e^x)^2

I can further simplify this expression by factoring out e^x from the numerator and denominator. This gives me:
F'(x) = 1 / (1 + e^x)

Therefore, the derivative of F(x) is F'(x) = \frac{1}{1 + e^x}.

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \), we'll use the **Quotient Rule**. The Quotient Rule states that if you have a function \( F(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

**Step 1: Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

Let:
- \( u(x) = e^x \)
- \( v(x) = 1 + e^x \)

**Step 2: Find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

The derivative of \( u(x) \) is:
\[
u'(x) = e^x
\]

The derivative of \( v(x) \) is:
\[
v'(x) = e^x
\]

**Step 3: Apply the Quotient Rule**

Substitute these into the Quotient Rule formula:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{e^x(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

Simplify the numerator:
\[
= \frac{e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x}}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]
\[
= \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

Notice that \( (1 + e^x) \) is a common term in the denominator. We can factor it out:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)(1 + e^x)}
\]
\[
= \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

However, we can further simplify this by recognizing that \( e^x \) in the numerator and one instance of \( (1 + e^x) \) in the denominator will cancel out:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{1}{1 + e^x}
\]

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \) is:

\[
\boxed{\frac{1}{1 + e^x}}

To find the derivative of \( \tan^{-1}(x) \), I start by recalling that this is the inverse tangent function, which can also be written as arctan(x).

Next, let’s set \( y = \tan^{-1}(x) \). This implies that \( \tan(y) = x \).

To differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \), we apply implicit differentiation. Differentiating \( \tan(y) \) gives \( \sec^2(y) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \), and the derivative of \( x \) is 1.

So, we have:
\[ \sec^2(y) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1 \]

Solving for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \), which represents the derivative \( \frac{d}{dx}(\tan^{-1}(x)) \), gives:
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2(y)} \]

Recall that \( \sec^2(y) = 1 + \tan^2(y) \). Since we know from the earlier step that \( \tan(y) = x \), substituting this in gives:
\[ \sec^2(y) = 1 + x^2 \]

Finally, substituting back into the derivative expression results in:
\[ \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2} \]
</think>

To find the derivative of \( \tan^{-1}(x) \), we'll use the rules of differentiation for inverse trigonometric functions.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Function:**
   - We are dealing with the inverse tangent function, written as \( y = \tan^{-1}(x) \).

2. **Differentiate Both Sides:**
   - Differentiate both sides of the equation with respect to \( x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} (y) = \frac{d}{dx} (\tan^{-1}(x))
     \]
   
3. **Apply Implicit Differentiation:**
   - Since \( y = \tan^{-1}(x) \), we can write:
     \[
     \tan(y) = x
     \]
   - Now, differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx} (\tan(y)) = \frac{d}{dx} (x)
     \]
   
4. **Differentiate Using Chain Rule:**
   - The derivative of \( \tan(y) \) with respect to \( y \) is \( \sec^2(y) \), and then we multiply by the derivative of \( y \) with respect to \( x \):
     \[
     \sec^2(y) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
     \]
   
5. **Solve for \( \frac{dy}{dx} \):**
   - Rearranging the equation gives:
     \[
     \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2(y)}
     \]
   
6. **Express in Terms of \( x \):**
   - Recall that \( y = \tan^{-1}(x) \), so we can use the identity \( \sec^2(y) = 1 + \tan^2(y) \).
   - Since \( \tan(y) = x \), substitute to get:
     \[
     \sec^2(y) = 1 + x^2
     \]
   
7. **Final Derivative:**
   - Substitute back into the derivative expression:
     \[
     \frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}
     \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\dfrac{1}{1+x^{2}}}

To find the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1} \), we will use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

**Step 1: Identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \)**

Let:
\[
u(x) = \sin x
\]
\[
v(x) = x^2 + 1
\]

**Step 2: Find the derivatives \( u'(x) \) and \( v'(x) \)**

Compute the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[
u'(x) = \cos x
\]
\[
v'(x) = 2x
\]

**Step 3: Apply the Quotient Rule**

Substitute \( u(x) \), \( u'(x) \), \( v(x) \), and \( v'(x) \) into the quotient rule formula:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} = \frac{\cos x (x^2 + 1) - \sin x (2x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

**Step 4: Simplify the Expression (if possible)**

In this case, no further simplification is needed.

**Final Answer**

\[
\boxed{\frac{(x^2 + 1)\cos x - 2x \sin x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}}
\]

To find the derivative of F(x) = x / (1 + x²), I will use the quotient rule. 

First, identify the numerator function u as x and the denominator v as 1 + x².

Next, compute their derivatives: u’ is 1 and v’ is 2x.

Apply the quotient rule formula: F’(x) = (u’v - uv’) / v².

Substitute the values into the formula to get F’(x) = [1*(1 + x²) - x*(2x)] / (1 + x²)².

Simplify the numerator: 1 + x² - 2x² becomes 1 - x².

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
\left( \frac{u}{v} \right)' = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2}
\]

Here, let:
- \( u(x) = x \)
- \( v(x) = 1 + x^2 \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
- \( u'(x) = 1 \)
- \( v'(x) = 2x \)

Now, apply the quotient rule:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{u'v - uv'}{v^2} = \frac{(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

Simplify the numerator:
\[
(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x) = 1 + x^2 - 2x^2 = 1 - x^2
\]

So, the derivative becomes:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \) is:
\[
\boxed{\dfrac{1 - x^{2}}{(1 + x^{2})^{2}}}
\]

The derivative of \(\sec x\) with respect to \(x\) can be found as follows:

1. Recognize that \(\sec x = \frac{1}{\cos x}\).
2. Apply the quotient rule or recall that the derivative of \(\frac{1}{u}\) is \(-\frac{u'}{u^2}\), where \(u = \cos x\) and \(u' = -\sin x\).
3. Substitute into the formula: 
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (\sec x) = \frac{\sin x}{\cos^2 x}
   \]
4. Simplify using trigonometric identities:
   \[
   \frac{\sin x}{\cos^2 x} = \sec x \tan x
   \]
